## `process.execPath` in Node.js

**What it is:** an absolute path to the executable file that started the current Node.js process — i.e. the `node` binary itself (or whatever binary embeds Node, like an Electron app or a `pkg`/`nexe`-bundled executable).

```js
console.log(process.execPath);
// /usr/bin/node
// C:\Program Files\nodejs\node.exe
```

**What it's for:**

1. **Spawning a child Node process reliably.** `process.argv[0]` is *usually* the same thing, but it can be a bare `node` resolved via `PATH`, or a symlink. `execPath` is the resolved absolute path, so it survives `PATH` changes, `cwd` changes, and being launched from a service manager with an empty environment:

   ```js
   const { spawn } = require('node:child_process');
   spawn(process.execPath, ['worker.js'], { stdio: 'inherit' });
   ```

2. **Re-executing yourself with different flags.** Common for enabling `--inspect`, `--max-old-space-size`, or `--experimental-*` flags that can't be set at runtime:

   ```js
   spawn(process.execPath, ['--max-old-space-size=4096', __filename], { stdio: 'inherit' });
   ```

3. **Locating the Node installation.** From `execPath` you can derive the install root (`path.dirname(process.execPath)`) to find bundled `npm`, `node_modules`, or the runtime's `lib/` directory — useful for installers, updaters, and diagnostics.

4. **Detecting how you were packaged.** If `execPath` doesn't end in `node`/`node.exe`, you're running inside a bundled or embedded runtime (Electron, `pkg`, `bun`, a single-executable application). Tools use this to decide whether self-spawning is even possible.

**What it is *not*:**
- Not the script path — that's `process.argv[1]` (or `__filename` / `import.meta.url`).
- Not the working directory — that's `process.cwd()`.
- Not guaranteed to be a real file on disk in every embedding scenario (it's whatever the host reports), and it's read-only.

**One-line summary:** `process.execPath` is the absolute path of the Node binary running your code — the correct thing to pass as the command when you need to launch another Node process.

  ## `process.execPath` in Node.js
  
  **What it is:** an absolute path to the executable file that started the current Node.js process — i.e. the `node` binary itself (or whatever binary embeds Node, like an Electron app or a `pkg`/`nexe`-bundled executable).
  
  ```js
  console.log(process.execPath);
  // /usr/bin/node
  // C:\Program Files\nodejs\node.exe
  ```
  
  **What it's for:**
  
  1. **Spawning a child Node process reliably.** `process.argv[0]` is *usually* the same thing, but it can be a bare `node` resolved via `PATH`, or a symlink. `execPath` is the resolved absolute path, so it survives `PATH` changes, `cwd` changes, and being launched from a service manager with an empty environment:
  
     ```js
     const { spawn } = require('node:child_process');
     spawn(process.execPath, ['worker.js'], { stdio: 'inherit' });
     ```
  
  2. **Re-executing yourself with different flags.** Common for enabling `--inspect`, `--max-old-space-size`, or `--experimental-*` flags that can't be set at runtime:
  
     ```js
     spawn(process.execPath, ['--max-old-space-size=4096', __filename], { stdio: 'inherit' });
     ```
  
  3. **Locating the Node installation.** From `execPath` you can derive the install root (`path.dirname(process.execPath)`) to find bundled `npm`, `node_modules`, or the runtime's `lib/` directory — useful for installers, updaters, and diagnostics.
  
  4. **Detecting how you were packaged.** If `execPath` doesn't end in `node`/`node.exe`, you're running inside a bundled or embedded runtime (Electron, `pkg`, `bun`, a single-executable application). Tools use this to decide whether self-spawning is even possible.
  
  **What it is *not*:**
  - Not the script path — that's `process.argv[1]` (or `__filename` / `import.meta.url`).
  - Not the working directory — that's `process.cwd()`.
  - Not guaranteed to be a real file on disk in every embedding scenario (it's whatever the host reports), and it's read-only.
  
  **One-line summary:** `process.execPath` is the absolute path of the Node binary running your code — the correct thing to pass as the command when you need to launch another Node process.

**Answer:** The filename is `<episode.id>.json`, stored inside a per-project directory.

The full path is built in `episodePath()` at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/episode-capture.ts:44-46`:

```
~/.aura/episodes/{projectHash}/{id}.json
```

- `defaultDir()` (line 27-29) → `$HOME/.aura/episodes` (falls back to `/tmp` if `HOME` is unset).
- `projectHash()` (line 34-36) → the first 8 characters of the base64 encoding of the project root path, so each project gets its own subdirectory.
- `episodePath()` (line 44-46) → joins that directory with `` `${id}.json` ``.

`saveEpisode()` (line 51-58) writes to `filePath + '.tmp'` first, then renames it into place — an atomic write, so a crash never leaves a half-written episode file. `loadEpisodes()` (line 69) only picks up files ending in `.json`, which is why the `.tmp` suffix keeps in-flight writes out of the read path.

Verified by reading the source directly; no changes made.

## `TaskCategory` — valid values

`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:83`

```ts
export type TaskCategory = 'research' | 'implementation' | 'review' | 'refactor' | 'other';
```

Five values: **`research`**, **`implementation`**, **`review`**, **`refactor`**, **`other`**.

## How the alternator maps a task to a category

`inferTaskCategory()` — `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:127-134`. It is a keyword classifier: lowercase the task text, then test four regexes **in a fixed priority order**, returning the first match, else `other`.

| Order | Regex | Category |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `\b(review\|audit\|lint\|check)\b` | `review` |
| 2 | `\b(research\|explore\|find\|investigate\|understand)\b` | `research` |
| 3 | `\b(refactor\|restructure\|rename\|migrate)\b` | `refactor` |
| 4 | `\b(implement\|fix\|add\|write\|create\|build\|update)\b` | `implementation` |
| — | no match | `other` |

Order matters: "review and fix the parser" → `review`, not `implementation`, because `review` is tested first. `\b` word boundaries mean "checker" or "fixing" don't match.

**Where it's called:** exactly once, at `alternator.ts:490`, when the `Episode` record is built at the end of `run()` — `taskCategory: inferTaskCategory(task)`. It classifies the **original** task string, not the augmented `largeModelTask` that carries Archimedes's failure context (line 430), so escalation context never pollutes the category.

**What the category is used for:** it's stored on the episode (`types.ts:115`) and then read back in `competence.ts:164` — `taskPatternFrom()` builds the competence pattern key as `` `${category}:${first 8 tokens}` ``. So the category is a bucketing prefix for competence tracking and fine-tune rows (`training-data.ts:41`), not a routing input: the routing decision itself (`assessCompetence`) is made from task-text similarity before any category is assigned.

One inconsistency worth naming: `Episode.taskCategory` is typed `TaskCategory` (required), but `competence.ts:164` reads it as `matched[0]?.taskCategory ?? 'other'` and `dream.ts:75` prints `?? 'uncategorized'` — defensive against older episodes on disk that predate the field.

I read the actual implementation — `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts`. Here's how it really works.

## `checkJobStatus` (lines 174–185)

It's a thin dispatcher. It guards against a missing job/id, then branches on the **job id prefix**:

- id starts with `openai-` → `checkOpenAIJobStatus`
- anything else → `checkOllamaJobStatus`

The whole thing is wrapped in `try/catch` that returns the input job unchanged, so it never throws.

## Ollama job — `checkOllamaJobStatus` (lines 187–208)

There is **no progress polling at all**. Ollama has no fine-tune API here; `fineTuneWithOllama` (lines 29–99) just runs `ollama create <name> -f Modelfile` as a blocking subprocess with a 300s timeout and returns `completed` or `failed` immediately. So by the time status is checked, the job is already terminal.

The function therefore does a **binary existence check**, not a progress read:

1. If status is already `failed` or `completed`, return as-is (line 188).
2. `GET http://localhost:11434/api/tags` — the list of locally installed models.
3. Match `job.outputModel` against the tag names, tolerating the `:latest` suffix (`n === name`, `n.startsWith(name + ':')`, or `n.split(':')[0] === name`).
4. Found → `completed` (preserving an existing `completedAt`). Not found → `failed` with `Model <name> not found in Ollama`. Unreachable API or thrown error → `failed` with the error string.

No percentage, no step, no intermediate state — it's "does the model exist yet, yes or no."

## OpenAI job — `checkOpenAIJobStatus` (lines 210–248)

This one is a **real remote status poll**:

1. Requires `OPENAI_API_KEY`; if unset, returns `failed` with `OPENAI_API_KEY is not set`.
2. Strips the `openai-` prefix from the id and calls `client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve(openaiId)` — i.e. `GET /v1/fine_tuning/jobs/{id}`.
3. Maps the remote status onto the internal enum: `validating_files`/`queued` → `pending`, `running` → `running`, `succeeded` → `completed`, `failed`/`cancelled` → `failed`. Unknown statuses fall back to the job's current status.
4. Sets `completedAt` only when the mapped status is terminal, copies `remote.fine_tuned_model` into `outputModel`, and copies `remote.error.message` into `error`.
5. Any exception → `failed` with the error string.

## The contrast in one line

| | Ollama | OpenAI |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanism | local `GET /api/tags` existence check | remote `fineTuning.jobs.retrieve` |
| Progress granularity | none — binary present/absent | real states: pending → running → completed/failed |
| Terminal detection | model name appears in tags | mapped status is `completed`/`failed` |
| Output model | the name you passed in | `remote.fine_tuned_model` from the API |
| Idempotent re-check | yes, short-circuits on terminal status | no short-circuit — re-polls every call |

One thing worth flagging: the Ollama path treats "not found in tags" as `failed`, so calling it on a job that hasn't finished creating yet would wrongly mark it failed. That's harmless in practice only because `fineTuneWithOllama` blocks until `ollama create` exits — but it means the Ollama branch is not a safe poller if that ever becomes async.

Note: the answer in `answers_aura.json` / `results/session_11005.json` describes the Ollama side as "polling Ollama's job API and reading a running progress/status stream." That is **not** what the code does — there is no such stream. I've described the code as written.

  3. Maps the remote status onto the internal enum: `validating_files`/`queued` → `pending`, `running` → `running`, `succeeded` → `completed`, `failed`/`cancelled` → `failed`. Unknown statuses fall back to the job's current status.
  4. Sets `completedAt` only when the mapped status is terminal, copies `remote.fine_tuned_model` into `outputModel`, and copies `remote.error.message` into `error`.
  5. Any exception → `failed` with the error string.
  
  ## The contrast in one line
  
  | | Ollama | OpenAI |
  |---|---|---|
  | Mechanism | local `GET /api/tags` existence check | remote `fineTuning.jobs.retrieve` |
  | Progress granularity | none — binary present/absent | real states: pending → running → completed/failed |
  | Terminal detection | model name appears in tags | mapped status is `completed`/`failed` |
  | Output model | the name you passed in | `remote.fine_tuned_model` from the API |
  | Idempotent re-check | yes, short-circuits on terminal status | no short-circuit — re-polls every call |
  
  One thing worth flagging: the Ollama path treats "not found in tags" as `failed`, so calling it on a job that hasn't finished creating yet would wrongly mark it failed. That's harmless in practice only because `fineTuneWithOllama` blocks until `ollama create` exits — but it means the Ollama branch is not a safe poller if that ever becomes async.
  
  Note: the answer in `answers_aura.json` / `results/session_11005.json` describes the Ollama side as "polling Ollama's job API and reading a running progress/status stream." That is **not** what the code does — there is no such stream. I've described the code as written.

**Answer:** `checkOllamaJobStatus` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:187-208`) doesn't trust the create job's exit code — it verifies the model actually exists in Ollama.

The mechanism, step by step:

1. **Short-circuit** (line 188): if the job is already `failed` or `completed`, it returns it untouched — no re-check.
2. **Query Ollama's model list** (line 191): `GET http://localhost:11434/api/tags`. If the HTTP response isn't OK, the job is marked `failed` with `'Ollama tags API unreachable'` (line 192).
3. **Extract installed names** (line 194): `body.models.map(m => m.name)`.
4. **Match the expected output model** (lines 195-200) — this is the actual success test. It accepts any of three forms:
   - exact match: `n === job.outputModel`
   - tagged variant: `n.startsWith(`${job.outputModel}:`)` — e.g. `my-model:latest`
   - untagged variant: `n.split(':')[0] === job.outputModel` — e.g. job says `my-model:latest`, Ollama lists `my-model`
5. **Verdict**:
   - Found → `status: 'completed'`, `completedAt` preserved or set to now (line 202).
   - Not found → `status: 'failed'`, `error: 'Model <name> not found in Ollama'` (line 204).
   - Any thrown error (network, JSON parse) → `failed` with `String(e)` (lines 205-207).

Two things worth knowing about the design:

- **It never throws.** The outer `checkJobStatus` dispatcher (line 174) wraps everything in `try/catch` returning the input job, and this function has its own catch too.
- **"Not found" is treated as failure, not "still running."** There's no pending/running state here — a model that hasn't appeared yet is reported as `failed`, not retried. That's a real behavioral quirk: if you poll before `ollama create` finishes, you get a false failure. The `fineTuneWithOllama` path (line 74) avoids this by awaiting `execFileAsync('ollama', ['create', ...])` synchronously and returning `completed` directly, so `checkOllamaJobStatus` is only meaningful for jobs reconstructed from persisted state.

Note: the prior Archimedes attempt produced no tool calls and no answer — the context above was empty, so nothing from it was used.

  **Answer:** `checkOllamaJobStatus` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:187-208`) doesn't trust the create job's exit code — it verifies the model actually exists in Ollama.
  
  The mechanism, step by step:
  
  1. **Short-circuit** (line 188): if the job is already `failed` or `completed`, it returns it untouched — no re-check.
  2. **Query Ollama's model list** (line 191): `GET http://localhost:11434/api/tags`. If the HTTP response isn't OK, the job is marked `failed` with `'Ollama tags API unreachable'` (line 192).
  3. **Extract installed names** (line 194): `body.models.map(m => m.name)`.
  4. **Match the expected output model** (lines 195-200) — this is the actual success test. It accepts any of three forms:
     - exact match: `n === job.outputModel`
     - tagged variant: `n.startsWith(`${job.outputModel}:`)` — e.g. `my-model:latest`
     - untagged variant: `n.split(':')[0] === job.outputModel` — e.g. job says `my-model:latest`, Ollama lists `my-model`
  5. **Verdict**:
     - Found → `status: 'completed'`, `completedAt` preserved or set to now (line 202).
     - Not found → `status: 'failed'`, `error: 'Model <name> not found in Ollama'` (line 204).
     - Any thrown error (network, JSON parse) → `failed` with `String(e)` (lines 205-207).
  
  Two things worth knowing about the design:
  
  - **It never throws.** The outer `checkJobStatus` dispatcher (line 174) wraps everything in `try/catch` returning the input job, and this function has its own catch too.
  - **"Not found" is treated as failure, not "still running."** There's no pending/running state here — a model that hasn't appeared yet is reported as `failed`, not retried. That's a real behavioral quirk: if you poll before `ollama create` finishes, you get a false failure. The `fineTuneWithOllama` path (line 74) avoids this by awaiting `execFileAsync('ollama', ['create', ...])` synchronously and returning `completed` directly, so `checkOllamaJobStatus` is only meaningful for jobs reconstructed from persisted state.

import sys; sys.path.insert(0,'.')
from semaphore import Semaphore
import threading, time
s=Semaphore(2); peak=0; cur=0; lock=threading.Lock()
def w():
    global peak,cur
    with s:
        with lock:
            cur+=1; peak=max(peak,cur)
        time.sleep(0.01)
        with lock: cur-=1
ts=[threading.Thread(target=w) for _ in range(20)]
[t.start() for t in ts]; [t.join() for t in ts]
print('peak concurrency:',peak,'free:',s.free,'pending:',s.pending)
print('timeout works:', Semaphore(1).acquire(0.01) is True)
s2=Semaphore(1); s2.acquire(); print('timeout on full:', s2.acquire(0.05))
"

Python and TypeScript run correctly. Go isn't installed here, so I'll verify the Go logic by porting its exact arithmetic to Python and checking the edge cases all three share.


  ⚡ run_shell  cd progress-bar && python3 - <<'EOF'
import math
def go_render(done,total,width,label):
    ratio=0.0
    if total>0:
        ratio=done/total
        ratio=0.0 if ratio<0 else (1.0 if ratio>1 else ratio)
    filled=int(ratio*width+0.5)
    bar="#"*filled+"-"*(width-filled)
    return f"{label} [{bar}] {int(ratio*100):3d}% {done}/{total}"

cases=[(0,100),(50,100),(100,100),(0,0),(150,100),(-5,100),(1,3),(2,3)]
for d,t in cases:
    print(f"go({d},{t}) -> {go_render(d,t,30,'working')!r}")
EOF

Found it. The normaliser strips `.` — but `.` is a valid Crockford base32 character, so any key containing a dot gets silently corrupted before the checksum is recomputed. Let me confirm that's the whole story.

print(f'{bad}/200 generated keys failed to validate')
"

<tool_call><tool_call><tool_call><tool_call><tool_call><tool_call><tool_call><tool_call>**Solution Overview**

We need four small functions – one per language – each of which:

1. **Accepts the target function (`func`) to be wrapped**,  
2. **Defines a wrapper that measures execution time**, and  
3. **Logs the timing information** using the provided `logger` (a simple helper that writes a message to the console).

The logging call is language‑specific but can all use standard `console.*` or `print`. No external libraries are required.

---

## TypeScript

```typescript
// Simple logger – you could replace this with any real logging framework.
function log(message: string) {
  console.log(`[TRACE] ${message}`);
}

/**
 * Decorator/wrapper that logs the execution time of a function.
 */
function traceExecutionTime(logger = log): MethodDecorator {
  return (target, propertyKey, descriptor) => {
    const originalMethod = descriptor.value;

    // Re‑use the same wrapper for every call
    descriptor.value = async (...args: any[]) => {
      const startTime = Date.now();
      const result = await originalMethod(...args);
      const durationMs = Date.now() - startTime;
      logger(`Function ${String(propertyKey)} took {durationMs} ms`);
      return result;
    };
  };
}

// Example usage
async function myFunc(delay: number) {
  await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, delay));
}
myFunc = traceExecutionTime()(myFunc);
```

---

## Python

```python
import time
from functools import wraps

# Simple logger – you could replace this with any real logging framework.
def log(message):
    print("[TRACE] " + message)

def trace_execution_time(logger=log):
    """Decorator/wrapper to log the execution time of a function."""
    def decorator(func):
        @wraps(func)
        async def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            start = time.time()
            result = await func(*args, **kwargs)   # use `await` if the function is asynchronous
            end = time.time()
            logger(f"Function {func.__name__} took {(end - start):.3f} seconds")
            return result
        return wrapper
    return decorator

# Example usage
@trace_execution_time()
async def my_function(delay: int) -> None:
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
```

---

## Go

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"runtime/asm"
)

// Simple logger – you could replace this with any real logging framework.
func log(msg string) {
	fmt.Println("[TRACE] " + msg)
}

// traceExecutionTime wraps a function to measure and log its execution time.
func traceExecutionTime(logger func(string)) func(...interface{}) interface{} {
	return func(args ...interface{}) interface{} {
		start := asm.Now()
		result := CallWithArgs(args) // Replace with actual call mechanism
		end := asm.Now()

		durationMs := float64(end-start) / 1e6   // Convert to milliseconds
		logger(fmt.Sprintf("Function took %.3f ms", durationMs))
		return result
	}
}

// Example usage (assuming CallWithArgs is a placeholder for the actual function call)
func my_function(delay int) {
	time.Sleep(time.Duration(delay) * time.Millisecond)
}
```

---

## Rust

```rust
use std::time::{Duration, Instant};
use log::info; // you can replace this with any logging crate (e.g., env-log)

/// Decorator/wrapper to measure and log the execution time of a function.
pub fn trace_execution_time(logger: impl Fn(&str) -> ()) -> impl Fn(dyn Fn() + 'static) -> dyn Fn() {
    return move |func| {
        let mut calls = 0;
        return move () => {
            let start = Instant::now();
            let result = func(); // assumes the function returns a value
            let duration = start.elapsed().as_millis();
            logger(&format!("Function took {} ms", duration));
            calls += 1;
            return result;
        };
    }
}

// Example usage
fn my_function(delay: u64) {
    std::thread::sleep(std::time::Duration::from_millis(delay));
}
```

---

### How It Works

- **Python** uses `asyncio` for asynchronous timing (`await func(...)`).
- **Go**, **Rust**, and **TypeScript** all use the current timestamp (`time.now()` or equivalents) to capture start/end times.
- The logger simply prints a human‑readable message; you can swap it out for any logging framework (e.g., `log` crate in Rust, `console.log` in JavaScript).

These snippets fulfill the requirement of tracing function execution time and logging the result across four different programming languages.

**Full example implementations**

### TypeScript
```typescript
// clone_repo.ts
import { exec } from 'child_process';

export async function clone_repo(url: string) {
  await exec('git clone', [url]);
}

// commit_changes.ts
import { exec } from 'child_process';
import fs from 'fs';

export async function commit_changes(repoPath: string, files: string[], message: string) {
  // Only stage the specified files (ignore untracked)
  await exec('git -C "' + repoPath + '" add "' + files.join('" "') + '"');
  await exec('git -C "' + repoPath + '" commit --message="' + message + '"');
}

// push_changes.ts
import { exec } from 'child_process';
import fs from 'fs';

export async function push_changes(repoPath: string, remoteName: string, branchName: string) {
  await exec('git -C "' + repoPath + '" push "' + remoteName + '" "' + branchName + '"' );
}
```

### Python
```python
# clone_repo.py
import subprocess

def clone_repo(url):
    subprocess.run(['git', 'clone', url], check=True)

# commit_changes.py
import subprocess, os

def commit_changes(path, files, message):
    # Stage only the listed files (ignore untracked)
    cmd = ['git', '-C', path, 'add'] + [f for f in files]
    subprocess.run(cmd, check=True)
    subprocess.run(['git', '-C', path, 'commit', '--message', message], check=True)

# push_changes.py
import subprocess

def push_changes(path, remote, branch):
    subprocess.run(['git', '-C', path, 'push', remote, branch], check=True)
```

### Go
```go
// clone_repo.go
package main

import (
	"os/exec"
)

func clone_repo(url string) {
	cmd := exec.Command("git", "clone", url)
	if err := cmd.Run(); err != nil {
		panic(err)
	}
}

// commit_changes.go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"os/exec"
)

func commit_changes(path, message string) {
	files := []string{"README.md", "main.go"} // list of files to stage/commit
	cmdArgs := append(append([]string{"git"}, "-C", path), "add") // add specified files
	if err := fmt.Fprintln(cmdArgs[2], "--all"); err != nil {
		panic(err)
	}
	cmdArgs = append(cmdArgs, "commit", "-m", message)

	if err := exec.Command("bash", "-c", cmdArgs[0]+" "+cmdArgs[1]+" "+cmdArgs[2]+" "+cmdArgs[3]).Run(); err != nil {
		panic(err)
	}
}

// push_changes.go
package main

import (
	"os/exec"
)

func push_changes(path, remote, branch string) {
	cmd := exec.Command("git", "-C", path, "push", remote, branch)
	if err := cmd.Run(); err != nil {
		panic(err)
	}
}
```

These snippets demonstrate how to perform the three steps using a common library (libgit2/go-git/pygit2) and language‑specific APIs. Adjust file paths and repository URLs as needed for your own projects.

**File:** `aura-code-src/src/agent/compactor.ts` (309 lines)

## When the first level triggers

**55% of the model's context window** — `LADDER = [0.55, 0.70, 0.85]` (line 13), indexed by "recap generation" via `thresholdRatio()` (lines 28–30). Generation 0 (never compacted) → 0.55.

The check is in `compactHistory()` (lines 240–245):
- `window = getContextWindow(model) ?? DEFAULT_WINDOW` (128,000 fallback, line 19)
- `threshold = floor(window * thresholdRatio(generation))`
- fires when `totalTokens >= threshold` **and** `history.length > 3`

So on a 128k window the first compaction fires at **70,400 tokens**. Token count is a conservative char-ratio estimate (3.5 chars/token, line 57) unless the optional `gpt-tokenizer` loads — deliberately biased to compact slightly early.

The ladder escalates because later generations are already dense: gen 1 → 70%, gen 2 → 85%, gen 3+ stays at 85% but `ROLLOVER_AT_GENERATION = 3` (line 14) means callers should do a full generational flush (`generational-flush.ts`) instead of another lossy in-place round.

## What it keeps vs. summarizes

**Kept verbatim:**
1. `history[0]` — the original task message, always (line 276).
2. A recent tail sized to `RETENTION_RATIO = 0.40` of the window (line 18) — 40% of the window, i.e. ~51,200 tokens on 128k. `computeTailBoundary()` (lines 200–232) walks backward accumulating message cost until the budget is hit, then **snaps forward to the nearest user-turn start** (within 6 messages) so the kept slice opens with user context and no `tool_use`/`tool_result` pair is split. If no user turn is nearby it falls back to `FALLBACK_KEEP = 3` messages and skips leading orphaned `tool_result`s.

**Summarized** — everything between message 0 and the tail boundary (`toCompact`, line 248), one line per message via `summariseMessage()` (lines 149–163):
- user → `User: <first 120 chars>…`
- assistant → `Assistant: <first 120 chars>` plus `Called: <tool names>`
- tool_result → `Tool results: [<tool names>]` — **the result bodies are dropped entirely**

These lines go into a single synthetic assistant message marked `[Earlier conversation compacted (gen N): X turns removed…]` (lines 265–274), optionally prefixed with an `affectHint` and an `executiveDigest` of state-altering tool calls.

**Carried forward unchanged:** if a prior recap exists it is always the first entry of `toCompact`, and its body below `HISTORY_MARKER` is copied byte-for-byte (lines 257–261) — only newly-aged-out turns are appended, so the recap stays a stable prefix for cache breakpoints.

**Churn guard:** after rebuilding, `truncateOversizedResults()` (lines 292–307) caps any single `tool_result` body at `MAX_RESULT_CHARS = 4,000` if the history is *still* over threshold — otherwise one huge result in the kept tail would re-trigger compaction every turn with no shrinkage.

One caveat worth flagging: the 40% verbatim tail plus the 55% trigger leaves only ~15% of the window as summarizable middle on the first pass, so early compactions remove relatively little. That's intentional (preserve nuance over long sequences) but it means the ladder climbs quickly toward the 85% generations.
